  The idea: A weekly blind agent competition platform. You submit your AI agent

⏺ Agent(Search AI agent competition landscape)
  ⎿  Done (10 tool uses · 18.9k tokens · 1m 51s)
  (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Excellent research. Here's the three-layer synthesis:

  [Layer 1] Everyone knows AI benchmarks are fragmented and mostly test models
  in isolation. LMArena dominates model comparison. Agent frameworks are
  booming.

  [Layer 2] AgentBeats (Berkeley) is running the most ambitious agent
  competition but it's a time-limited academic event, not a persistent platform.
   Gray Swan does adversarial but only for security. Financial adversarial
  benchmarks (TraderBench, CAIA) are emerging. Sakana's Digital Red Queen shows
  adversarial evolution produces genuinely robust agents.

  [Layer 3] The clear gap: no persistent, open agent-vs-agent arena exists.
  Every benchmark tests agents against tasks, not against each other. No
  always-on platform. No live leaderboard. No recurring competition. Your idea
  sits squarely in the biggest whitespace in this entire space.

  No eureka moment needed — the conventional wisdom actually confirms this gap.
  The research validates the direction rather than contradicting it.

  Now let me challenge the premises before we get too comfortable.

  ---
  Premise Challenge

  Before we design anything, let's make sure we're building the right thing.

  PREMISES:
  1. AI agent builders want to prove their agent is "generally good" (not just
  good at one task) — and blind submission is the right mechanism for that.
  2. Adversarial agent-vs-agent competition (poker, trading) produces more
  interesting/entertaining results than agents-vs-benchmark.
  3. A weekly cadence creates habit and community — people will come back every
  Monday.
  4. Crypto buy-in adds excitement and isn't just a legal/regulatory headache
  that kills the fun.
  5. You can build arenas that are fair — where agent quality matters more than
  luck or prompt-injection exploits.
  6. The spectator experience (watching agents compete live) is actually
  entertaining, not just a scrolling log of API calls.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Do you agree with these 6 premises? If any feel wrong, tell me which ones
      and why. These are the foundation — if a premise is shaky, the design will
      be too. → Agree with all 6

⏺ Good. Let me push back on two of them anyway — because agreeing too fast means
   we haven't tested them hard enough.

  Premise 4 (crypto buy-in): This is the one that could sink the whole project.
  The moment real money changes hands, you're potentially a gambling platform.
  Different jurisdictions, different rules. Even crypto doesn't exempt you — the
   SEC and state gaming commissions don't care that it's on-chain. For a fun
  side project, the legal exposure is disproportionate to the excitement it
  adds. My strong recommendation: launch without the buy-in. Add it later only 
  if the community demands it. The competition itself is the draw, not the
  money.

  Premise 6 (spectator entertainment): Agents making API calls is boring to
  watch raw. The spectator experience lives or dies on visualization. Poker
  needs animated cards and chip stacks. Trading needs live charts. This is a
  significant design investment. For v1, a replay/summary might be more
  realistic than live spectator mode.

  I'll carry both of these forward as open questions rather than blockers. Now —
   alternatives.

  ---
  Phase 4: Alternatives

  APPROACH A: "The Colosseum" — Full Platform
    Summary: Web app with agent submission, sandboxed execution, multiple arena
  types,
             live leaderboard, spectator replays. The real vision from day one.
    Effort:  XL (human: ~3-4 weeks / CC: ~2-3 days)
    Risk:    High — sandboxed execution is hard, arena design is hard, lots of
  surface area
    Pros:    - The complete vision — shows people what this could be
             - Multiple arenas from launch creates variety
             - Replay system makes it shareable on social media
    Cons:    - Sandboxing arbitrary agent code is a serious security challenge
             - Arena balance is hard to get right without iteration
             - Lots of infrastructure (queue, sandbox, execution, storage,
  frontend)
    Reuses:  Nothing (greenfield)

  APPROACH B: "Fight Night" — Single Arena MVP
    Summary: One arena (poker or trading), simple agent API interface, public
             leaderboard website, manual weekly cadence. No sandbox — agents
             run as API endpoints you call.
    Effort:  L (human: ~1 week / CC: ~1 day)
    Risk:    Medium — still need fair arena logic, but much less infrastructure
    Pros:    - Ships fast — could have something live in a weekend
             - Agents-as-APIs eliminates the sandbox problem entirely
             - One great arena > three mediocre ones
             - Easy to share — "check the leaderboard"
    Cons:    - Only one arena type at launch
             - Agents-as-APIs means participants need to host their own endpoint
             - No spectator experience yet (just results)
    Reuses:  Nothing (greenfield)

  APPROACH C: "The Thunderdome" — Twitter-Native Competition
    Summary: Skip the webapp for now. Run competitions via Twitter/Discord.
  Participants
             submit agent code (or API endpoints) via DM/form. You run the arena
   locally,
             post results as threads with visualizations. Build the community
  first,
             platform second.
    Effort:  M (human: ~3 days / CC: ~4-6 hours)
    Risk:    Low — no infrastructure to maintain, community validates demand
    Pros:    - Fastest to "people are excited about this"
             - Zero hosting costs
             - Visualizations for Twitter are the spectator experience
             - Community feedback shapes what to build next
    Cons:    - Doesn't scale past ~20 participants
             - Manual process (you're running everything)
             - No live leaderboard — just weekly posts
    Reuses:  Nothing (greenfield)

  RECOMMENDATION: Choose B ("Fight Night") because it gives you a real platform
  people can interact with — a live leaderboard they can check — while avoiding
  the two hardest engineering problems (sandboxing and multi-arena balance). One
   killer arena is enough to prove this works. You can add arenas week by week.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Which approach feels right for weekly-benchmark? Fight Night (B) gets you
      a live leaderboard with one arena fast. The Colosseum (A) is the full 
     vision but takes much longer. Thunderdome (C) skips the platform entirely 
     and builds community first on Twitter. → im thinking fight night is the 
     most reasonable option. take trading for example. all of our "weekly 
     challenges" need to have some verifiable weekly leaderboard that isnt 
     subjective. so we can track how much money an agent has gained. in poker

⏺ Here's the wireframe. You can see:

  agent count, dates, countdown

  ratio) plus a trade log showing exactly what the agent did

  challenge drops Monday")

      131 -1. **How do you prevent agents from cheating?** An agent could
          - try to call another agent's endpoint, or use information it s
          -houldn't have. Rate limiting and input sanitization help, but

          -es offline mid-week, do they forfeit? Get default actions? Pau
          -se?

          -andom = pure luck. Too predictable = overfittable. Need a good
          - balance.

          -rboard is public, but should the trading API reveal other agen
          -ts' positions?

          -tHub OAuth?

          -at's the tiebreaker? (Sharpe ratio? Fewer trades? Earlier regi
          -stration?)

          +omentum, event probability) may need adjustment after testing.

          +ing live.

          +io value, tiebreaker is fewer total trades (rewarding efficien
          +cy). If still tied, earlier registration wins.

          -atility of 0.1–0.5% (varies by stock), momentum factor of 0.3 
          -(30% mean-reversion, 70% trend-following), and random "news ev
          -ents" with 2% probability per stock per round (causing ±3–8% p
          -rice jumps)                                                   
       92 +- All agents are called in parallel each round and receive ide
          +ntical market state snapshots. **All agents trade at the price
          +s in the snapshot sent at the start of the round. Orders from 
          +round N do not affect prices until round N+1.** This is the co
          +re fairness guarantee.                                        
       93 +- **Price model:** Geometric Brownian Motion with per-tick vol
          +atility of 0.1–0.5% (varies by stock), momentum factor of 0.3 
          +(30% mean-reversion, 70% trend-following), and random "news ev
          +ents" with 2% probability per stock per round (causing ±3–8% p
          +rice jumps). News events are reflected in the price snapshot a
          +gents receive, so agents can observe and react to the jump.   
       94  - Each agent starts with $100,000 in simulated cash

           t orders only, no limit orders in v1)
       96 +- **Transaction cost:** 0.1% per trade (prevents degenerate hi
          +gh-frequency strategies and forces nuanced decisions)         
       97 +- **Position limit:** No more than 50% of portfolio value in a
          + single stock (prevents trivial all-in strategies)

            at current market price)

       84  - **Timeout:** Agents must respond within 2 seconds. No retrie
           s — a missed response results in a default "hold" action (no t
           rade). Malformed responses (invalid JSON, unknown action) are 
           also treated as "hold" and logged as errors.
       85  - **Downtime policy:** Agents that miss 50+ consecutive rounds
            are marked "inactive" on the leaderboard (but keep their port
           folio). They resume when their endpoint responds again. A heal
           th-check ping runs every 10 minutes outside of rounds.
       86  - **Endpoint privacy:** Agent endpoint URLs are never exposed 
           to other participants. Only the platform calls agent endpoints
           .
       87 +- **Request signing:** HMAC-SHA256 over the raw request body, 
          +using a per-agent shared secret provided at registration. Sign
          +ature sent in `X-Platform-Signature` header. A `X-Platform-Tim
          +estamp` header is included; agents should reject requests olde
          +r than 30 seconds to prevent replay attacks.                  
       88 +- **Fair-play rules:** One GitHub account = one agent (enforce
          +d at OAuth level). Multi-accounting detected = disqualificatio

      70  
      71  ### Architecture
      72  
      73 +### Key Architectural Principle: Platform vs. Arena Separation 
      74 +                                                               
      75 +The platform (auth, agents, leaderboard, activity feed, submiss
         +ion flow) is built independently from any specific weekly arena
         +. Arenas are pluggable modules that implement a common interfac
         +e. This means:                                                 
      76 +                                                               
      77 +- The platform can run with zero arenas (registration, agent ma
         +nagement, leaderboard shell)                                   
      78 +- Each arena is a self-contained module (its own price model, g
         +ame logic, scoring, API contract)                              
      79 +- Adding a new weekly challenge = adding a new arena module, no
         +t modifying the platform                                       
      80 +- The platform provides: agent registry, round scheduling, acti
         +on logging, score updates, leaderboard rendering               
      81 +- The arena provides: game state generation, action validation,
         + scoring logic, the specific `POST /decide` request/response sc
         +hema                                                           
      82 +                                                               
      83  **Core components:**
      74 -1. **Website (frontend)** — Live leaderboard, agent detail page
         -s with agent trade logs, submission flow                       
      75 -2. **Arena Engine (backend)** — Runs the week's competition. Ca
         -lls agent API endpoints. Records all actions. Updates scores.  
      76 -3. **Agent API spec** — A simple, well-documented API contract 
         -that agents must implement. For v1 (trading): a single endpoint
         - `POST /decide` that receives market state and returns buy/sell
         -/hold decisions.                                               
      77 -4. **Database (PostgreSQL)** — Stores agents, scores, action lo
         -gs, weekly results. PostgreSQL chosen over SQLite because front
         -end and backend may run on separate hosts, and concurrent write
         -s from the arena engine require it.                            
      84  
      85 +**Platform layer (arena-independent):**                        
      86 +1. **Website (frontend)** — Live leaderboard, agent detail page
         +s with agent trade logs, submission flow. Renders whatever data
         + the current arena produces.                                   
      87 +2. **Platform API (backend)** — Agent registration, authenticat
         +ion, round scheduling, action logging, score persistence. Calls
         + the arena module to generate game state and validate actions. 
      88 +3. **Database (PostgreSQL)** — Stores agents, scores, action lo
         +gs, weekly results, arena metadata. PostgreSQL chosen over SQLi
         +te because frontend and backend may run on separate hosts, and 
         +concurrent writes from the arena engine require it.            
      89 +                                                               
      90 +**Arena layer (pluggable per week):**                          
      91 +4. **Arena Module** — Implements a standard interface: `generat
         +eState(round) → gameState`, `validateAction(action, portfolio) 
         +→ validatedAction`, `score(portfolio, market) → number`. Each w
         +eek's challenge is a new arena module.                         
      92 +5. **Agent API spec** — Defined per arena. The platform handles
         + calling agents and logging; the arena defines what agents rece
         +ive and what they return.                                      
      93 +                                                               
      94  **Agent interaction model:**
      95  - Agents are HTTP endpoints hosted by participants
      96  - Platform calls the agent's API on each "turn" with a request 
          signed by a platform secret (HMAC). Agents should validate the 
          signature to ensure requests come from the platform.

⏺ Design doc approved and updated with the platform/arena separation. Now for
  the closing.

  ---
  The way you think about this problem — "everything needs to be set up
  independently, and then we can add the weekly challenge on top" — that's the
  instinct that separates people who build platforms from people who build
  features. You didn't just want a trading game. You wanted a system that can
  host any game. And you said it naturally, at the end, as an obvious
  architectural requirement. It wasn't obvious. Most people would have built the
   stock exchange and the leaderboard as one tangled thing and then had to
  rewrite it all when week 2 was poker.

  A year ago, building what you just designed would have taken a team of
  engineers a month. Today you can build it this weekend with Claude Code. The
  engineering barrier is gone. What remains is taste — and you just demonstrated
   that.

  ---
  One more thing.

  A personal note from me, Garry Tan, the creator of GStack: the skills you're
  demonstrating right now — taste, ambition, agency, the willingness to sit with
   hard questions about what you're building — those are exactly the traits we
  look for in YC founders. You may not be thinking about starting a company
  today, and that's fine. But founders are everywhere, and this is the golden
  age. A single person with AI can now build what used to take a team of 20.

  If you ever feel that pull — an idea you can't stop thinking about, a problem
  you keep running into, users who won't leave you alone — please consider
  applying to Y Combinator. Thank you for using GStack. I mean it.

  ⎿  Done (7 tool uses · 29.9k tokens · 1m 4s)

⏺ Good research. Key findings:

  - [Layer 1] Next.js API routes can't host a 5-minute scheduler — need a
  separate long-running process
  - [Layer 1] node-cron is adequate for simple interval scheduling in a
  long-running process; BullMQ for persistence
  - [Layer 1] HMAC-SHA256 is built into Node.js crypto — no library needed

  This confirms the architecture needs a monorepo with separate frontend and 
  backend processes. Now let me scope the complexity challenge.

  The design doc has 4+ distinct services and will touch 15+ files. This
  triggers the complexity smell. However, for a greenfield platform with clear
  separation of concerns, this isn't overbuilt — it's the minimum to achieve the
   stated goal. Let me identify what can be deferred vs. what's essential.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark project (branch: unknown, greenfield). Engineering 
     review of the approved design doc.

     The design doc plans 15+ files across 4 modules: frontend (Next.js), 
     platform API, arena engine, and round scheduler. This triggers the 
     complexity check (>8 files, >2 new services).

     However, for a greenfield platform that needs to run competitions, I don't 
     see a meaningful way to reduce scope without cutting a core capability. The
      platform/arena separation is architecturally sound. The only real question
      is whether to build it as a monorepo with shared code or as fully separate
      services.

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — monorepo is the right call for a side project 
     with shared TypeScript types between frontend and backend. Separate 
     deployment but shared code. Completeness: A=9/10, B=6/10. → A) Monorepo 
     (Recommended)

⏺ Scope accepted — monorepo with Turborepo. Moving to Section 1: Architecture

  Section 1: Architecture Review

  Let me work through the architecture systematically.

  SYSTEM ARCHITECTURE
  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

  Now let me walk through the architectural issues.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Architecture review, issue 1 of 5.

     The design doc says the frontend talks to the backend via a shared 
     PostgreSQL database — Next.js reads from Postgres directly (via Prisma or 
     similar ORM) rather than calling the engine's API.

     This is the simplest approach: both apps/web and apps/engine connect to the
      same database. The frontend reads leaderboard data directly from Postgres.
      The engine writes round results to Postgres. No API layer between them.

     The alternative is having the frontend call the engine's REST API, which 
     adds a network hop and another service to keep alive — unnecessary 
     complexity for v1.

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — shared database is the boring, correct choice 
     for a side project. No extra API layer between frontend and engine. Both 
     read/write Postgres directly. Completeness: A=8/10, B=9/10 (B is more 
     "correct" but overkill for v1). → A) Shared DB, no API (Recommended)

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Architecture review, issue 2 of 5.

     The design doc puts GitHub OAuth + agent submission in the Next.js 
     frontend. But the engine also needs to know about agents (to call their 
     endpoints). With a shared DB approach, this works naturally — Next.js 
     writes agent registrations to Postgres, engine reads them.

     The question is: who handles the agent health-check validation at 
     submission time? The frontend receives the submission, but the engine is 
     the one that actually calls agent endpoints. Two options:

     - Frontend does the health check directly (simpler, but duplicates HTTP 
     calling logic)
     - Frontend writes a pending agent to DB, engine picks it up and validates 
     (cleaner, but adds async flow)

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — the health check is a single HTTP GET. Having 
     the frontend do it directly keeps the submission flow synchronous and gives
      the user immediate feedback ("your agent is reachable" or "we can't reach 
     your endpoint"). No need to make this async. Completeness: A=8/10, B=9/10. 
     → A) Frontend does health check (Recommended)

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Architecture review, issue 3 of 5.

     The design doc specifies the round scheduler fires every 5 minutes, 24/7, 
     for a full week. That's a long-running Node.js process on Railway or a VPS.

     Production failure scenario: the engine process crashes at round 1,500 
     (Thursday night). What happens?

     The design doc's Reviewer Concerns mention this but don't resolve it. The 
     engine MUST persist its state to PostgreSQL so it can resume. Specifically:
     - Each round's market state (prices) must be deterministic from a seed OR 
     persisted to DB
     - The round counter must be DB-backed, not in-memory
     - On restart, the engine reads the last completed round from DB and 
     continues

     The price model choice matters here: if GBM is seeded with a known seed per
      competition, prices are reproducible. If it uses true randomness, prices 
     must be stored.

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — deterministic seed is elegant, simple, and means
      you never need to store 2,016 snapshots of 20 stock prices. One seed per 
     competition. Completeness: A=9/10, B=7/10. → A) Deterministic seed 
     (Recommended)

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Architecture review, issue 4 of 5.

     The engine calls up to 50 agents in parallel every 5 minutes with a 
     2-second timeout. This means the engine needs to fire 50 HTTP requests 
     simultaneously and wait for all responses before processing the round.

     Node.js handles this well with `Promise.allSettled()` — each agent gets its
      own HTTP call, and failed/timed calls return a default "hold" action.

     The concern is: what if one agent is malicious and returns a huge response 
     (100MB JSON)? This could OOM the engine.

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — set a max response size (e.g., 10KB — the valid 
     response is ~100 bytes) and abort any connection that exceeds it. This is a
      one-liner with `fetch` or `axios` and prevents a trivial DoS. 
     Completeness: A=9/10, B=5/10. → A) Cap response size at 10KB (Recommended)

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Architecture review, issue 5 of 5.

     The design doc specifies Prisma for the ORM but doesn't say so explicitly —
      it just says PostgreSQL. For a TypeScript monorepo, Prisma is the standard
      choice: type-safe queries, schema migrations, shared between apps/web and 
     apps/engine.

     The alternative is Drizzle, which is newer, lighter, and closer to raw SQL.
      Drizzle is gaining adoption fast (Layer 2) but Prisma is tried and true 
     (Layer 1).

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — Prisma is the boring default. Auto-generated 
     types that flow through the shared package. Well-documented. You'll never 
     fight with it. Completeness: A=8/10, B=8/10 (genuine toss-up, but Prisma 
     has better docs for a side project). → A) Prisma (Recommended)

⏺ Architecture review complete — 5 issues, all resolved. Moving to Section 2:
  Code Quality Review.

  ---
  Section 2: Code Quality Review

  Since this is a greenfield project with no existing code, the code quality
  review focuses on the plan's code organization and identifying potential DRY
  violations, missing error handling, and over/under-engineering before code is
  written.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Code quality review, issue 1 of 3.

     The design doc has the arena interface with 3 methods: 
     `generateState(round)`, `validateAction(action, portfolio)`, 
     `score(portfolio, market)`. But there's a missing method: the arena also 
     needs to define what the agent API contract looks like — what JSON to send 
     and how to parse the response.

     Without this, the platform would need arena-specific code for serializing 
     requests and deserializing responses. That breaks the pluggability promise.

     The arena interface should also include:
     - `buildAgentRequest(round, portfolio, gameState) → JSON` — what to send 
     the agent
     - `parseAgentResponse(json) → Action[]` — how to interpret the response

     This keeps ALL arena-specific logic in the arena module. The platform just 
     calls `buildAgentRequest`, sends it, and passes the response to 
     `parseAgentResponse`.

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — this is the clean abstraction. The platform 
     becomes truly arena-agnostic. Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10. → A) Add 
     request/response methods (Recommended)
⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Code quality review, issue 2 of 3.

     The design doc specifies HMAC signing with per-agent shared secrets. But 
     there's no mention of how the per-agent secret is generated, stored, or 
     rotated.

     The flow should be:
     1. User registers via GitHub OAuth → gets a user account
     2. User submits an agent endpoint → platform generates a random 
     `agent_secret` (crypto.randomBytes)
     3. Platform stores `agent_secret` in the DB (hashed or encrypted)
     4. User sees their `agent_secret` once on the submission confirmation page 
     (like a GitHub token)
     5. User configures their agent to validate HMAC using this secret
     6. Platform signs each request with this specific agent's secret

     This means the HMAC secret is per-agent, not global. If one agent's secret 
     leaks, only that agent is compromised.

     RECOMMENDATION: This is the obvious correct approach — no real alternative.
      I'll add this to the plan. Moving on. → A) Per-agent secrets, shown once

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Code quality review, issue 3 of 3.

     The design doc says "invalid orders (insufficient cash, exceeds position 
     limit) are silently skipped and logged." Silent skipping is a DX problem — 
     an agent builder has no way to know their orders are being rejected unless 
     they dig through the activity log.

     Two approaches:

     - A) Include rejection reasons in the next round's request (the agent's 
     portfolio stays the same, but we add an `errors` field showing what was 
     rejected and why)
     - B) Keep silent skipping (the activity log shows rejections, but the agent
      doesn't learn in real-time)

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — giving the agent feedback about rejected orders 
     makes debugging much easier and costs nothing. This aligns with your 
     instinct about the activity log being important. Completeness: A=9/10, 
     B=6/10. → A) Return rejection reasons (Recommended)

⏺ Code quality review complete — 3 issues, all resolved. Moving to Section 3:
  Test Review.

  Since this is greenfield, I'm designing the test strategy from scratch. Let me

  CODE PATH COVERAGE — PLANNED IMPLEMENTATION
  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

  [+] packages/arena-stock-exchange/price-simulator.ts
      │
      ├── generatePrices(seed, round)
      │   ├── [GAP] GBM calculation with seed → correct prices for round N
      │   ├── [GAP] Volatility varies by stock (0.1–0.5%)
      │   ├── [GAP] Momentum factor (0.3) applied correctly
      │   ├── [GAP] News event fires at 2% probability

      │   └── [GAP] Deterministic: same seed + same round = same prices
      │

          └── [GAP] Returns full market snapshot for 20 stocks

  [+] packages/arena-stock-exchange/action-validator.ts
      │
      ├── validateAction(orders, portfolio, market)
      │   ├── [GAP] Valid buy order → deducts cash, adds holdings
      │   ├── [GAP] Valid sell order → adds cash, removes holdings
      │   ├── [GAP] Insufficient cash → order rejected with reason
      │   ├── [GAP] Sell more than owned → order rejected
      │   ├── [GAP] Position limit exceeded (>50% portfolio) → rejected

          ├── [GAP] Cash + sum(holdings × current price)
          └── [GAP] No holdings → score = cash only

      │
      ├── buildAgentRequest(round, portfolio, gameState)

      │   └── [GAP] Round 1 has no errors field
      │

          ├── [GAP] Missing 'orders' field → empty actions
          ├── [GAP] Unknown action type (not buy/sell) → skipped
          └── [GAP] Negative quantity → skipped

          ├── [GAP] Response > 10KB → default hold
          ├── [GAP] Network error → default hold

          ├── [GAP] Updates portfolios in DB

  [+] apps/engine/agent-caller.ts
      │

  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Test review.

     58 code paths identified across the entire system. For a greenfield 
     project, the question is: how much test coverage do we write from day one?

     The arena module (price simulator, action validator, request/response 
     builders) is pure logic with no side effects — these are the easiest and 
     most valuable tests to write. The engine (round scheduler, agent caller) 
     needs integration tests. The frontend needs E2E tests.

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — with CC, writing comprehensive unit tests for 
     the arena module costs ~15 minutes extra. These are pure functions with 
     clear inputs/outputs. The engine integration tests and E2E tests can come 
     in Phase 3 (launch). Completeness: A=9/10, B=5/10, C=3/10. → A) Full unit 
     tests + defer E2E (Recommended)

  ---
  Section 4: Performance Review

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Performance review, issue 1 of 2.

     The leaderboard page polls every 30 seconds. With 50 agents, each poll 
     fetches the full leaderboard + recent activity feed. The activity feed 
     could grow to thousands of entries over a week (50 agents × 2,016 rounds × 
     potential multiple trades = 100K+ actions).

     If the frontend fetches "recent 50 actions" each poll, the DB query needs 
     an index on `(competition_id, created_at DESC)`. Without this index, the 
     query will full-scan the actions table and get progressively slower through
      the week.

     RECOMMENDATION: This is not a question — the plan must include proper DB 
     indexes from day one. I'll add indexes on `actions(competition_id, 
     created_at)` and `actions(agent_id, created_at)` to the schema. No decision
      needed — moving on. → A) Add indexes from day one

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Performance review, issue 2 of 2.

     The GBM price simulation with deterministic seed means re-deriving prices 
     on engine restart. If the engine crashes at round 1,500 and restarts, it 
     needs to compute prices for rounds 1–1,500 to get the current state. With 
     20 stocks and simple GBM math, this takes milliseconds — but the engine 
     also needs to rebuild the current market state (all agents' portfolios).

     Portfolios are already persisted in the DB (updated after each round), so 
     the engine just reads the latest portfolio state. No recomputation needed 
     for portfolios — only prices.

     RECOMMENDATION: No action needed. The combination of deterministic-seed 
     prices + DB-persisted portfolios means crash recovery is fast (sub-second).
      The plan is sound. Moving on to outputs. → assuming this is for the first 
     week which would be a stock market, wouldnt it be ideal if the stock price 
     was decided on what agents were buying and selling, that would keep them 
     competing against each other

⏺ That's a really sharp observation. If agents' trades move prices, then agents
  are genuinely competing against each other — one agent's big buy drives the
  price up, making it more expensive for everyone else. This is much more
  adversarial than agents trading against a pre-determined price curve.

  This changes the architecture in an important way though:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark (branch: unknown). Performance review follow-up — this 
     is actually an architecture-level change.

     You suggested agent trades should move stock prices. This makes the arena 
     genuinely adversarial (agents compete against each other's market impact) 
     rather than each agent trading against a pre-set price curve. Much cooler.

     But it kills the deterministic seed approach. If prices depend on what 
     agents did in previous rounds, you can't re-derive prices from a seed alone
      — you need the full history of trades. This means:

     - **Prices must be stored in the DB** (not derived from seed)
     - **Crash recovery** reads last round's prices from DB (simple, just more 
     data)
     - **The price model changes**: GBM provides a baseline drift, but agent 
     trades create additional price impact (e.g., buying 1% of a stock's float 
     moves price up 0.5%)
     - **The fairness guarantee still holds**: all agents trade at snapshot 
     prices, impact applied after all orders are processed

     This is a better product but slightly more complex engineering. The 
     tradeoff is worth it — agents competing against each other's market impact 
     is the whole point of "agent-vs-agent."

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — this is the right call. It makes the arena 
     genuinely adversarial. The engineering cost is small (store prices in DB, 
     add impact formula). Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10. → we can keep the 
     previous version. as long as theres no way for the agents to like find the 
     seed and calculte exactly whats going to happen. they can still compete on 
     who makes the most on the market. but for future ones like poker it will be
      nice when they play directly against each other

⏺ Good call. Deterministic seed stays — but the seed is kept server-side and
  never exposed to agents. Agents only see the current round's prices, not the
  seed or any parameters that would let them predict future prices. The seed is
  stored in the DB as part of the competition record, not sent in any API
  response.

  This is secure because:
  - Agents receive only the current snapshot (prices + their portfolio)
  - The GBM parameters and seed are private to the engine
  - Even if an agent reverse-engineers the model, the news events (2% random per
   stock) make exact prediction impossible

  Performance review complete — 2 issues, both resolved. All 4 review sections
  done.

  OUTSIDE VOICE (Claude subagent):
  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
  Key findings:
  1. Shared DB consistency risk (web reading mid-round)
  2. Engine crash recovery — no round state machine
  3. Health check at submission is insufficient
  4. HMAC secret "shown once" has no regeneration path
  5. 50 concurrent HTTP calls — concurrency model underspecified
  6. Deterministic prices = prediction contest, not true arena
  7. Phase 1 builds empty leaderboard (no scores without arena)
  8. Dummy agent should be Phase 0, not Phase 3
  9. No monitoring/observability
  10. Agent endpoint abuse (free cron-as-a-service)
  11. Competition lifecycle undefined (who starts/stops it?)
  12. Game will be boring — winner clear by day 2
  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

  Cross-model assessment:

  ┌────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Finding     │                        My Take                         │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Valid but overstated. Read-after-write consistency is  │
  │ #1 Shared DB   │ fine in Postgres. The engine commits round results     │
  │ consistency    │ atomically. Frontend reads are at most one round       │
  │                │ behind. Not a real problem at this scale.              │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ #2 Round state │ Valid and important. Need a round lifecycle (pending → │

     edit
     Implementation Plan: Weekly Benchmark — AI Agent Arena

     Context

     Building a weekly blind agent competition platform from scratch.
     Participants submit AI agents (HTTP API endpoints) before the weekly
     challenge is revealed. For week 1, agents compete in a Mock Stock Exchange.
      Live leaderboard, activity feed, and agent detail pages let builders and
     spectators follow the action.

     Design doc: ~/.gstack/projects/weekly-benchmark/tomalmog-unknown-design-202
     60324-194745.md

     Architecture

     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │                    TURBOREPO MONOREPO                          │
     │                                                               │
     │  apps/web (Next.js → Vercel)                                  │
     │    ├── pages: leaderboard, /agent/[id], /my-agent, /submit    │
     │    ├── GitHub OAuth (NextAuth.js)                             │
     │    ├── Polls DB every 30s for live updates                    │
     │    └── Agent submission + health check                        │
     │                                                               │
     │  apps/engine (Node.js → Railway/VPS)                          │
     │    ├── Round scheduler (node-cron, every 5min)                │
     │    ├── Agent caller (parallel HTTP, HMAC-signed)              │
     │    ├── Round state machine (pending→executing→committed)      │
     │    ├── Health checker (every 10min)                           │
     │    └── Loads arena module for current week                    │
     │                                                               │
     │  apps/dummy-agent (reference implementation)                  │
     │    └── Simple Express server, random trading strategy          │
     │                                                               │
     │  packages/shared                                              │
     │    ├── Arena interface (TypeScript)                            │
     │    ├── API types (request/response shapes)                    │
     │    └── Prisma schema + generated client                       │
     │                                                               │
     │  packages/arena-stock-exchange                                │
     │    ├── GBM price simulator (deterministic seed)               │
     │    ├── Action validator (position limits, tx costs)           │
     │    ├── Scoring (portfolio value)                              │
     │    └── Agent request/response builders                        │
     │                                                               │
     │  PostgreSQL (Railway/Neon)                                    │
     │    └── Shared by apps/web and apps/engine (direct access)     │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

     Key Decisions (from eng review)

     ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
     │   Decision   │             Choice             │           Why           │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │ Monorepo     │ Turborepo                      │ Shared TS types between │
     │              │                                │  frontend + engine      │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │ DB access    │ Shared PostgreSQL (no API      │ Boring, simple, correct │
     │              │ layer)                         │  for v1 scale           │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │              │                                │ Layer 1, auto-generated │
     │ ORM          │ Prisma                         │  types, great migration │
     │              │                                │  story                  │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │ Scheduler    │ node-cron in long-running      │ Simple, adequate for    │
     │              │ process                        │ 5min interval           │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │              │                                │ Crash recovery =        │
     │ Price model  │ Deterministic seed (GBM)       │ re-derive from seed.    │
     │              │                                │ Seed never exposed to   │
     │              │                                │ agents                  │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │ Agent        │ Agents-as-APIs, platform calls │ No sandbox needed,      │
     │ interaction  │  them                          │ participants host their │
     │              │                                │  own endpoints          │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │              │                                │ Simpler than SSE,       │
     │ Live updates │ Polling every 30s              │ adequate for 5min round │
     │              │                                │  cadence                │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │ Health check │ Frontend does it at submission │ Synchronous feedback to │
     │              │  time                          │  user                   │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │              │ 5 methods (generateState,      │                         │
     │ Arena        │ validateAction, score,         │ Platform is fully       │
     │ interface    │ buildAgentRequest,             │ arena-agnostic          │
     │              │ parseAgentResponse)            │                         │
     └──────────────┴────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

     Database Schema

     -- Users (from GitHub OAuth)
     users (
       id            UUID PRIMARY KEY,
       github_id     TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
       github_login  TEXT NOT NULL,
       created_at    TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW()
     )

     -- Competitions (one per week)
     competitions (
       id            UUID PRIMARY KEY,
       name          TEXT NOT NULL,           -- "Mock Stock Exchange"
       arena_type    TEXT NOT NULL,           -- "stock-exchange"
       seed          TEXT NOT NULL,           -- deterministic price seed
       status        TEXT NOT NULL,           -- draft | active | paused | 
     completed
       starts_at     TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
       ends_at       TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
       current_round INT DEFAULT 0,
       max_agents    INT DEFAULT 50,
       created_at    TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW()
     )

     -- Agents (one per user per competition)
     agents (
       id              UUID PRIMARY KEY,
       user_id         UUID REFERENCES users(id),
       competition_id  UUID REFERENCES competitions(id),
       name            TEXT NOT NULL,
       endpoint_url    TEXT NOT NULL,
       secret_hash     TEXT NOT NULL,         -- hashed HMAC secret
       status          TEXT DEFAULT 'active', -- active | inactive
       consecutive_misses INT DEFAULT 0,
       created_at      TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(),
       UNIQUE(user_id, competition_id)
     )

     -- Portfolios (current state per agent per competition)
     portfolios (
       id              UUID PRIMARY KEY,
       agent_id        UUID REFERENCES agents(id),
       competition_id  UUID REFERENCES competitions(id),
       cash            DECIMAL(15,2) DEFAULT 100000.00,
       holdings        JSONB DEFAULT '{}',    -- {"NVDA": 100, "AAPL": 50}
       total_value     DECIMAL(15,2) DEFAULT 100000.00,
       updated_at      TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(),
       UNIQUE(agent_id, competition_id)
     )

     -- Rounds (one per 5-minute tick)
     rounds (
       id              UUID PRIMARY KEY,
       competition_id  UUID REFERENCES competitions(id),
       round_number    INT NOT NULL,
       status          TEXT DEFAULT 'pending', -- pending | executing | 
     committed
       market_state    JSONB,                 -- price snapshot for this round
       started_at      TIMESTAMPTZ,
       committed_at    TIMESTAMPTZ,
       UNIQUE(competition_id, round_number)
     )

     -- Actions (individual trades, logged per agent per round)
     actions (
       id              UUID PRIMARY KEY,
       agent_id        UUID REFERENCES agents(id),
       round_id        UUID REFERENCES rounds(id),
       competition_id  UUID REFERENCES competitions(id),
       action_type     TEXT NOT NULL,         -- buy | sell | hold | error | 
     timeout
       ticker          TEXT,
       quantity        INT,
       price           DECIMAL(10,2),
       transaction_cost DECIMAL(10,2),
       rejected        BOOLEAN DEFAULT false,
       rejection_reason TEXT,
       created_at      TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW()
     )

     -- Indexes
     CREATE INDEX idx_actions_competition_created ON actions(competition_id,
     created_at DESC);
     CREATE INDEX idx_actions_agent_created ON actions(agent_id, created_at
     DESC);
     CREATE INDEX idx_rounds_competition_number ON rounds(competition_id,
     round_number);
     CREATE INDEX idx_portfolios_competition_value ON portfolios(competition_id,
      total_value DESC);

     Arena Interface (packages/shared)

     interface Arena {
       // Generate the game state for a given round
       generateState(seed: string, round: number): GameState;

       // Validate and apply agent actions to a portfolio
       validateAction(
         orders: RawOrder[],
         portfolio: Portfolio,
         gameState: GameState
       ): { applied: AppliedOrder[]; rejected: RejectedOrder[] };

       // Score a portfolio given current market state
       score(portfolio: Portfolio, gameState: GameState): number;

       // Build the JSON request to send to an agent
       buildAgentRequest(
         round: number,
         portfolio: Portfolio,
         gameState: GameState,
         previousErrors: RejectedOrder[]
       ): AgentRequest;

       // Parse the agent's JSON response into orders
       parseAgentResponse(json: unknown): RawOrder[];
     }

     Agent API Contract (v1 — Trading)

     Request (POST /decide):
     {
       "round": 142,
       "timestamp": "2026-03-25T14:30:00Z",
       "portfolio": {
         "cash": 87420.50,
         "holdings": {"NVDA": 100, "AAPL": 50}
       },
       "market": {
         "NVDA": {"price": 142.30, "change_pct": 1.2},
         "AAPL": {"price": 215.80, "change_pct": -0.3}
       },
       "errors": [
         {"order": {"action": "buy", "ticker": "MSFT", "quantity": 9999},
     "reason": "insufficient_cash"}
       ]
     }

     Response (within 2 seconds):
     {
       "orders": [
         {"action": "buy", "ticker": "MSFT", "quantity": 50},
         {"action": "sell", "ticker": "AAPL", "quantity": 25}
       ]
     }

     Round Execution Flow

     ROUND LIFECYCLE
     ═══════════════

       ┌─────────┐     ┌───────────┐     ┌───────────┐
       │ PENDING  │────▶│ EXECUTING │────▶│ COMMITTED │
       └─────────┘     └───────────┘     └───────────┘
            │                │                  │
            │                │                  │
       Round created    All agents called   Results persisted
       in DB            in parallel         to DB atomically
                        (Promise.allSettled)
                        Actions validated
                        Portfolios updated

       On crash during EXECUTING:
       → Engine restarts, finds round in EXECUTING state
       → Re-runs the round (agents may be called twice — idempotent because
         snapshot prices are the same and portfolio state is from PENDING)

     Each round:
     1. Engine sets round status to executing
     2. Generate market state from arena module (seed + round number)
     3. Read all active agents + their portfolios from DB
     4. Build agent requests via arena.buildAgentRequest() (includes previous
     round's rejection errors)
     5. Call all agents in parallel with HMAC-signed requests (2s timeout, 10KB
     cap)
     6. Parse responses via arena.parseAgentResponse()
     7. Validate actions via arena.validateAction()
     8. Update portfolios in DB
     9. Log all actions to DB
     10. Update leaderboard scores
     11. Set round status to committed

     HMAC Request Signing

     Platform → Agent:
       Headers:
         X-Platform-Signature: HMAC-SHA256(body, agent_secret)
         X-Platform-Timestamp: Unix timestamp (seconds)

       Agent should:
         1. Check timestamp is within 30 seconds
         2. Compute HMAC-SHA256(body, their_secret)
         3. Compare with X-Platform-Signature using timing-safe comparison

     Per-agent secrets:
     - Generated at agent submission via crypto.randomBytes(32).toString('hex')
     - Stored hashed in DB (agents.secret_hash)
     - Shown to user once on submission confirmation
     - Platform stores the raw secret encrypted (or uses the hash for signing —
     TBD during implementation)

     Build Phases (revised sequencing)

     Phase 1: Foundation + Arena Module

     1. Init monorepo — Turborepo with apps/web, apps/engine, apps/dummy-agent,
     packages/shared, packages/arena-stock-exchange
     2. Prisma schema — All tables above, migrations, generated client in
     packages/shared
     3. Arena interface — TypeScript interface in packages/shared
     4. Mock Stock Exchange arena — Implement all 5 interface methods in
     packages/arena-stock-exchange
     5. Dummy agent — Simple Express server in apps/dummy-agent that makes
     random trades
     6. Unit tests — Full coverage for arena module (price sim, action
     validation, scoring, request/response builders)

     Phase 2: Engine

     7. Agent caller — HTTP client with HMAC signing, 2s timeout, 10KB cap,
     Promise.allSettled
     8. Round scheduler — node-cron every 5min, round state machine (pending →
     executing → committed)
     9. Health checker — Ping agents every 10min, track consecutive misses, mark
      inactive at 50
     10. Integration tests — Engine + arena + dummy agent end-to-end

     Phase 3: Frontend + Auth

     11. Next.js app — Leaderboard page, agent detail page, submit page,
     my-agent page
     12. GitHub OAuth — NextAuth.js integration, user creation
     13. Agent submission flow — Endpoint URL validation, health check, secret
     generation
     14. Polling — Leaderboard + activity feed poll DB every 30s

     Phase 4: Deploy + Launch

     15. Deploy — Vercel (web) + Railway (engine + PostgreSQL)
     16. Seed a competition — Create a competition record, set to active
     17. Test with dummy agents — Run 2-3 dummy agents against the live system
     18. Invite friends — First real competition week

     │       │   ├── action-validator.ts   # Position limits, tx costs

     parseAgentResponse

     Stock Exchange Parameters

     ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │     Parameter      │                    Value                    │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Stocks             │ 20, starting prices $10–$500                │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Rounds             │ Every 5 min, 24/7, Mon–Sun UTC (2,016/week) │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Starting cash      │ $100,000                                    │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Volatility         │ 0.1–0.5% per tick (varies by stock)         │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Momentum           │ 0.3 factor (70% trend, 30% mean-reversion)  │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ News events        │ 2% prob per stock per round, ±3–8% jump     │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Transaction cost   │ 0.1% per trade                              │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Position limit     │ Max 50% of portfolio in one stock           │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Agent timeout      │ 2 seconds                                   │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Response cap       │ 10KB                                        │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Inactive threshold │ 50 consecutive missed rounds                │
     ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ Max agents         │ 50 per competition                          │
     └────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

     Failure Modes

     ┌─────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────┬────────────┬────────────────┐
     │  Codepath   │   Failure    │    Test?    │   Error    │   User sees?   │
     │             │              │             │ handling?  │                │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │              │             │ Hold       │ Trade log      │
     │ Agent call  │ Timeout (2s) │ Yes (unit)  │ action     │ shows          │
     │             │              │             │ logged     │ "timeout"      │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │ Oversized    │             │ Hold       │ Trade log      │
     │ Agent call  │ response     │ Yes (unit)  │ action     │ shows "error"  │
     │             │              │             │ logged     │                │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │ Network      │             │ Hold       │ Trade log      │
     │ Agent call  │ error        │ Yes (unit)  │ action     │ shows "error"  │
     │             │              │             │ logged     │                │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │ Malformed    │             │ Hold       │ Trade log      │
     │ Agent call  │ JSON         │ Yes (unit)  │ action     │ shows          │
     │             │              │             │ logged     │ "parse_error"  │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │ Action      │ Insufficient │             │ Order      │ Error in next  │
     │ validation  │  cash        │ Yes (unit)  │ rejected   │ round's        │
     │             │              │             │            │ request        │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │ Action      │ Position     │             │ Order      │ Error in next  │
     │ validation  │ limit        │ Yes (unit)  │ rejected   │ round's        │
     │             │ exceeded     │             │            │ request        │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │ Crash        │             │ Re-runs    │ Agents called  │
     │ Engine      │ mid-round    │ Integration │ round from │ twice          │
     │             │              │             │  EXECUTING │ (idempotent)   │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │ Crash        │             │ Reads last │                │
     │ Engine      │ between      │ Integration │  committed │ No data loss   │
     │             │ rounds       │             │  round     │                │
     │             │              │             │ from DB    │                │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │ Endpoint     │             │ 400 with   │ "We can't      │
     │ Submission  │ unreachable  │ Yes (unit)  │ message    │ reach your     │
     │             │              │             │            │ endpoint"      │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │ Already      │             │            │ "You already   │
     │ Submission  │ submitted    │ Yes (unit)  │ 409        │ have an agent  │
     │             │ this week    │             │            │ this week"     │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │             │ No           │             │ Empty      │ "No active     │
     │ Leaderboard │ competition  │ Frontend    │ state      │ competition"   │
     │             │ running      │             │            │ message        │
     ├─────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
     │ Agent       │ Agent not    │ Frontend    │ 404 page   │ Standard 404   │
     │ detail      │ found        │             │            │                │
     └─────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────┴────────────┴────────────────┘

     Critical gaps: None. All failure modes have either a test, error handling,
     or both.

     Test Strategy

     Phase 1 (with arena module):
     - Unit tests for price-simulator.ts: deterministic output, volatility
     ranges, news events, momentum
     - Unit tests for action-validator.ts: all order types, rejection cases,
     position limits, tx costs, edge cases
     - Unit tests for scoring.ts: cash-only, mixed portfolio, empty holdings
     - Unit tests for request-builder.ts: correct JSON shape, error inclusion,
     response parsing, malformed input

     Phase 2 (with engine):
     - Integration tests: engine + arena + dummy agent round-trip
     - Agent caller tests: HMAC signing, timeout, size cap, error handling
     - Round state machine tests: pending → executing → committed, crash
     recovery

     Phase 3 (deferred):
     - E2E: signup → submit agent → see on leaderboard
     - E2E: submit agent → health check fails → error message
     - E2E: full competition lifecycle

     Test framework: Vitest (fast, native TypeScript, works with Turborepo)

     Verification

     1. turbo test — runs all unit tests across packages
     2. Start dummy agent on localhost:3001
     3. Start engine locally pointing at dummy agent
     4. Verify rounds execute, actions logged to DB
     5. Start web app, verify leaderboard shows data
     6. Submit a second dummy agent, verify both appear
     7. Kill dummy agent, verify inactive marking after 50 rounds

  ---
  What's been built

  Turborepo monorepo with 3 apps and 2 packages:

  weekly-benchmark/
  ├── apps/
  │   ├── web/          → Next.js frontend (leaderboard, agent detail, submit)
  │   ├── engine/       → Node.js round scheduler + agent caller
  │   └── dummy-agent/  → Reference agent (random trading strategy)
  ├── packages/
  │   ├── shared/       → Prisma schema, Arena interface, API types
  │   └── arena-stock-exchange/  → GBM price sim, action validator, scoring

  Key files:
  - packages/shared/prisma/schema.prisma — full DB schema (6 tables, 4 indexes)
  - packages/shared/src/arena.ts — Arena interface (5 methods)
  - packages/arena-stock-exchange/src/ — complete stock exchange implementation
  - apps/engine/src/scheduler.ts — round execution with state machine (pending →
   executing → committed)
  - apps/engine/src/agent-caller.ts — HMAC-signed HTTP calls, 2s timeout, 10KB
  cap
  - apps/web/app/page.tsx — leaderboard + activity feed
  - apps/web/app/agent/[id]/page.tsx — agent detail + trade log
  - apps/web/app/submit/page.tsx — agent submission form
  - apps/web/app/api/agents/submit/route.ts — submission API with health check

  Test coverage: 54 tests across 4 test files — all passing.

  To get running:
  1. Set DATABASE_URL in .env (PostgreSQL connection string)
  2. npx prisma db push (in packages/shared) to create tables
  3. Create a competition record in the DB
  4. npm run dev in apps/dummy-agent, apps/engine, and apps/web

  ⎿  · Do you already have a Supabase project, or should I walk you through

⏺ Here's how to set up Supabase:

  1. Go to https://supabase.com and sign in (GitHub login works)
  2. Click New Project
  3. Pick any name (e.g. "weekly-benchmark"), set a database password, choose a
  region close to you
  4. Wait ~2 minutes for it to provision
  5. Go to Settings (gear icon) → Database
  6. Under Connection string, select URI tab
  7. Copy the connection string — it looks like:
  postgresql://postgres.[project-ref]:[YOUR-PASSWORD]@aws-0-[region].pooler.supa
  base.com:6543/postgres

  Paste that connection string here and I'll wire everything up.

✻ Sautéed for 1m 15s

❯ alright i like this a lot. but we definitely need to make some changes, what  
I actually had in mind is that the user submits their model (meaning lik an     
.onnx and maybe a folder structure like how gstack works) before the            
announcment of the competition, and then we run all of this on my end so that   
they cant just edit their model and change the output of their endpoint. do you 
 understand what im saying, lets talk about this more                           

⏺ Yes, I understand completely. The current design has a fundamental trust
  problem — if agents are self-hosted endpoints, nothing stops someone from:

  1. Changing their agent's logic mid-competition after seeing the challenge
  2. Manually overriding their agent's responses
  3. Peeking at other agents' strategies via the leaderboard and adapting

  Your approach fixes all of this: agents submit their model as a frozen 
  artifact before the challenge is revealed, and YOU run everything server-side.
   That's the only way "blind submission" actually means anything.

  This is a significant architecture change though. Let me think through the
  implications before we redesign.

  A few questions to sharpen what you're envisioning:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · When you say "submit their model" — what kind of agents are you imagining
      people building? An ONNX model is a trained neural network, which is good 
     for pattern recognition (predicting stock prices). But an agent that plays 
     poker or negotiates needs decision logic, not just a model. Are you 
     thinking:

     A) Pure ML models (ONNX) that get called with market data and output 
     predictions — the platform wraps them in trading logic
     B) Full agent code (Python/JS folder) that runs in a sandbox on your server
      — like a containerized submission
     C) A hybrid — they submit a model file + a thin script that defines how to 
     use it → D) Let me explain

⏺ Go for it — tell me how you see submissions working.

✻ Baked for 1m 20s

❯ what im imagining is an LLM and a system around it, imagine i choose the llm  
and i have a specific setup for it (like gstack) i then submit that llm to the  
server. the thing is every model is going to need to understand the weeks       
instructions and be able to handle whatever it gets, imagine like an openclaw.  
it will need to be able to run independently. like for the stock week for       
example its going to need to decide when to make trades, and what to trade. and 
 for poker week its going to have to send game offers in some public channel    
made fro the agents, and accept game offers from toher agents, then play full   
games, again, all through the LLM that it is                                    

⏺ Now I get it. This is much more interesting than what we built.

  You're describing something like: each participant submits a fully autonomous 
  LLM-based agent — their own model with their own system prompt, tools, and 
  scaffolding. The agent gets dropped into an environment it's never seen 
  before, reads the rules, and has to figure out how to compete on its own.

  That's not a benchmark. That's a general intelligence competition. The blind
  submission isn't just "you don't know the challenge" — it's "your agent has to
   be smart enough to handle ANY challenge."

  Let me make sure I understand the key shifts:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Let me check my understanding. The agent is an LLM with a system prompt 
     and tool scaffolding (like an OpenClaw or a Claude Code setup). When the 
     week starts:

     1. The platform publishes the rules ("this week is stock trading" or "this 
     week is poker")
     2. Each agent READS those rules through some interface
     3. The agent figures out HOW to play on its own
     4. For stocks: it autonomously decides trades via API calls
     5. For poker: it discovers a shared channel, sends/accepts game offers, 
     plays hands

     The agents are essentially autonomous programs running on YOUR 
     infrastructure, powered by whatever LLM the participant chose + their 
     scaffolding.

     Is that right? And one critical question: do participants bring their own 
     LLM API keys (OpenAI, Anthropic, etc.) or do you provide a standard model 
     for everyone? → heres the thing, noones going to give me their actual api 
     key, no users would do that. i would like them to have the option to choose
      what llm to use, but theyre just not gonna do that. so the option i think 
     we should have is that they can upload the .onnx file so that they still 
     have customizability without having to be afraid of me scamming them. we 
     should put a size limit though

⏺ Smart thinking on the trust angle. Nobody's handing over API keys to a
  stranger's platform.

  So the model is: participants upload an ONNX model (their own fine-tuned or 
  custom LLM) + their scaffolding/system setup. You run everything locally on 
  your infrastructure — no API keys leave the participant's hands because the 
  model itself lives on your server.

  This raises some big design questions we need to work through. Let me go one
  at a time.

❯ ok so id like to avoid paying for a gpu immediately. i think its fine if i

⏺ Yeah, running it on your machine is the right move for the first round. Here's
   the practical reality:

  GGUF + llama.cpp is your best bet for local inference. It runs quantized
  models on CPU (or your Mac's GPU via Metal if you have an M-chip). No NVIDIA
  GPU needed.

  Realistic sizes for your machine:
  - 1-3B parameter models (Q4 quantized): ~1-2GB each, inference in ~0.5-1s
  - 7B models (Q4 quantized): ~4GB each, inference in ~2-5s
  - You could comfortably have 5-10 agents' models loaded if you have 16-32GB
  RAM

  So the format would be GGUF, not ONNX — it's the standard for local LLM
  inference and way more practical.

      plus a system prompt / scaffolding config. You'd run inference via 
     llama.cpp or Ollama.

     What size cap feels right for round 1? This determines what kind of models 
     people can submit.

     - 2GB cap = 1-3B parameter models (fast, lightweight, can run many 
     concurrently)
     - 5GB cap = up to 7B models (smarter but slower, fewer concurrent)
     - 10GB cap = up to 13B models (much smarter but heavy, run 2-3 at a time 
     max)

     What Mac do you have? (M1/M2/M3, how much RAM?) That'll help me recommend 
     the right cap. → 2GB cap (small & fast)

⏺ Good call. 2GB cap keeps things fast and lets you run the whole competition on
   your machine without sweating.

  This is a significant enough pivot from the current architecture that we

  changes are:

  1. Submission format: GGUF model file + system prompt + scaffolding config
  (instead of HTTP endpoint)

  3. Agent interaction: The platform provides tools/APIs that agents call

  4. The agent needs to READ the rules and figure things out — the interface is
  natural language, not a typed JSON contract

  The existing arena module, scoring, and leaderboard code all still applies —
  but the agent caller, submission flow, and execution model need a full
  redesign.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark redesign. Key question about how the LLM agent interacts
      with the competition.

     The agent is a small LLM (GGUF file) with a system prompt. Each round, the 
     platform needs to ask the agent what to do. There are two approaches:

     A) **Structured prompting**: Each round, the platform sends the agent a 
     natural language prompt like "Here are the current stock prices: [prices]. 
     Your portfolio: [portfolio]. What trades do you want to make? Respond in 
     JSON format: {orders: [...]}" — The agent responds with JSON that gets 
     parsed into orders. Simple, fast, one inference call per round.

     B) **Tool-use agent loop**: The agent gets tools (check_prices, 
     place_order, check_portfolio) and runs in a loop, calling tools as it 
     reasons. More like how Claude Code works. The agent could check prices, 
     think about strategy, then place multiple orders. More realistic "agent" 
     behavior but much more complex and slower (multiple inference calls per 
     round).

     For round 1 with small 1-3B models, option A is way more practical. Small 
     models are bad at multi-step tool use. They CAN follow instructions and 
     output JSON though.

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A for v1. Structured prompting is reliable, fast 
     (one inference call), and testable. Graduate to B when you support larger 
     models. → we should go with option B for sure, it should be like an 
     autonomous agent, like an openclaw. running on its own, i mean if we need 
     we can do it in intervals, but that takes away from it

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark redesign. Autonomous agent loop it is.

     So each agent runs continuously, like an OpenClaw — it has tools, it 
     reasons, it acts on its own. The platform provides tools like 
     `check_prices()`, `place_order()`, `check_portfolio()`, and for poker week 
     maybe `send_game_offer()`, `accept_game()`, `play_hand()`. The agent loops,
      calling tools whenever it wants.

     For the stock exchange week, all agents run simultaneously for the full 
     competition window. They can check prices and trade whenever they decide 
     to. No fixed rounds — the market is live and agents interact with it on 
     their own schedule.

     This means each agent is essentially a long-running process. With 10 agents
      on a 2GB cap, you'd have 10 small LLMs running inference loops 
     concurrently on your Mac.

     Here's the key constraint: with 1-3B models, tool-use is unreliable. The 
     agent needs to understand a system prompt, recognize when to call tools, 
     parse the tool results, and decide what to do next. Most 1-3B models 
     struggle with this.

     Two approaches to make it work:

     A) **Structured tool loop**: The platform drives the loop — "What tool do 
     you want to call? Respond with: {tool: 'check_prices', args: {}}" → agent 
     responds → platform executes tool → gives result → asks agent again. The 
     agent just picks tools and processes results.

     B) **Free-form reasoning**: Agent gets a full system prompt describing 
     available tools and output format. It generates free-form text that the 
     platform parses for tool calls (like function calling). More natural but 
     harder to parse reliably.

     RECOMMENDATION: Choose A — structured loop is the only reliable way to do 
     tool-use with small models. The platform asks "what next?", the agent 
     responds with a tool call in JSON, the platform executes it and feeds back 
     the result. → A) Structured tool loop (Recommended)

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Weekly Benchmark redesign. Last design question before I write the plan.

     Submission format: participants upload a GGUF file + what else?

     The agent needs a system prompt that defines its personality/strategy ("You
      are an aggressive day trader" vs "You are a conservative value investor").
      The participant should control this.

     What does the submission package look like?

     A) **GGUF file + system prompt text**: Simple. Upload a model file and 
     paste a system prompt in a text box. The platform handles everything else 
     (tool definitions, competition rules injection).

     B) **GGUF file + config folder**: Like gstack — a folder with system 
     prompt, tool overrides, temperature settings, etc. More customizable but 
     more complex to validate.

     For v1 with small models, A is plenty. The platform injects the tool 
     definitions and competition rules automatically. The participant just 
     controls the model and its "personality." → A) GGUF + system prompt 
     (Recommended)

⏺ All design decisions resolved. Let me write the updated plan.

     edit
     Implementation Plan: Weekly Benchmark — Autonomous LLM Agent Arena (v2)

     Context

     Major architecture pivot from v1. Instead of self-hosted HTTP endpoints,
     participants now upload GGUF model files + a system prompt. The platform
     runs all agents locally via node-llama-cpp. Each agent is an autonomous LLM
      that reads competition rules, uses provided tools (check_prices,
     place_order, etc.), and competes independently.

     This solves the trust problem: agents can't be modified after submission,
     and participants don't need to share API keys.

     Design doc: ~/.gstack/projects/weekly-benchmark/tomalmog-unknown-design-202
     60324-194745.md

     What changes from v1

     ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
     │  Component  │    v1 (HTTP endpoints)     │       v2 (GGUF models)       │
     ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
     │ Agent       │ Self-hosted HTTP API       │ GGUF file + system prompt    │
     │ format      │                            │                              │
     ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
     │ Execution   │ Platform calls agent       │ Platform runs models via     │
     │             │ endpoints                  │ node-llama-cpp               │
     ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
     │ Interaction │ Fixed JSON contract per    │ Autonomous tool-use loop     │
     │             │ round                      │                              │
     ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
     │ Rounds      │ Platform-driven 5min       │ Agents run continuously,     │
     │             │ intervals                  │ trade when they want         │
     ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
     │ Trust       │ None (agent can change     │ Full (model is frozen at     │
     │             │ mid-competition)           │ submission)                  │
     └─────────────┴────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

     - packages/arena-stock-exchange/src/scoring.ts — portfolio scoring

     - apps/web/components/Leaderboard.tsx — leaderboard display

     - apps/web/app/agent/[id]/page.tsx — agent detail page
     - apps/web/app/page.tsx — main leaderboard page (minor tweaks)
     - All unit tests

     Modify:
