     - apps/web/app/submit/page.tsx — file upload instead of URL
     - apps/web/app/api/agents/submit/route.ts — file handling instead of health
      check
     - apps/engine/src/scheduler.ts — model runner instead of HTTP calls

     Delete:
     - apps/engine/src/agent-caller.ts — replaced by model runner
     - apps/engine/src/health-checker.ts — no longer needed

     New:
     - apps/engine/src/model-runner.ts — loads GGUF, runs structured tool loop
     - apps/engine/src/tool-executor.ts — executes tool calls against arena
     state
     - apps/engine/src/agent-loop.ts — orchestrates the agent's autonomous loop

     Architecture

     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │                    WEEKLY BENCHMARK v2                         │
     │                                                               │
     │  apps/web (Next.js → Vercel)                                  │
     │    ├── Upload GGUF file (2GB cap) + system prompt             │
     │    ├── Leaderboard, agent detail, activity feed               │
     │    └── GitHub OAuth (future)                                  │
     │                                                               │
     │  apps/engine (Node.js → your Mac)                             │
     │    ├── Competition Manager                                    │
     │    │   ├── Loads all agent GGUF models via node-llama-cpp     │
     │    │   ├── Starts agent loops (one per agent, concurrent)     │
     │    │   └── Manages shared market state                        │
     │    │                                                          │
     │    ├── Agent Loop (per agent)                                 │
     │    │   ├── System prompt: participant's prompt + tool defs    │
     │    │   │   + competition rules                                │
     │    │   ├── Structured tool loop:                              │
     │    │   │   1. "What tool do you want to call?" → agent        │
     │    │   │   2. Agent responds with JSON tool call              │
     │    │   │   3. Platform executes tool, returns result           │
     │    │   │   4. Repeat until agent calls "wait" or "done"       │
     │    │   └── Rate limited: max 1 tool call per second           │
     │    │                                                          │
     │    ├── Market Simulator                                       │
     │    │   ├── Price updates every 30 seconds (background)        │
     │    │   └── Shared state: all agents see same prices           │
     │    │                                                          │
     │    └── Tool Executor                                          │
     │        ├── check_prices() → current market prices             │
     │        ├── check_portfolio() → agent's cash + holdings        │
     │        ├── place_order(action, ticker, quantity) → result     │
     │        ├── check_leaderboard() → current rankings             │
     │        └── wait(seconds) → pause before next action           │
     │                                                               │
     │  packages/shared — Prisma schema, types                       │
     │  packages/arena-stock-exchange — price sim, validation        │
     │                                                               │
     │  Storage: GGUF files saved to disk (./uploads/)               │
     │  Database: PostgreSQL (Supabase)                              │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

     Agent Execution Model

     AGENT LIFECYCLE
     ═══════════════

       Competition starts
            │
            ▼
       Load GGUF model via node-llama-cpp
            │
            ▼
       Create chat session with:
         - Participant's system prompt
         - Tool definitions (injected by platform)
         - Competition rules (injected by platform)
            │
            ▼
       ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
       │  STRUCTURED TOOL LOOP            │
       │                                  │
       │  Platform: "You are in a stock   │
       │  trading competition. Available  │
       │  tools: [list]. What would you   │
       │  like to do? Respond with JSON:  │
       │  {tool: '...', args: {...}}"     │
       │         │                        │
       │         ▼                        │
       │  Agent responds: JSON tool call  │
       │         │                        │
       │         ▼                        │
       │  Platform executes tool          │
       │         │                        │
       │         ▼                        │
       │  Platform: "Result: [result].    │
       │  What would you like to do       │
       │  next?"                          │
       │         │                        │
       │         ▼                        │
       │  (repeat)                        │
       └──────────────────────────────────┘
            │
       Competition ends → dispose model

     Tools (Stock Exchange Week)

     const TOOLS = {
       check_prices: {
         description: "Get current prices for all stocks",
         args: {},
         returns: "Object with ticker → {price, change_pct}"
       },
       check_portfolio: {
         description: "Get your current cash balance and stock holdings",
         args: {},
         returns: "{cash: number, holdings: {ticker: quantity}, totalValue: 
     number}"
       },
       place_order: {
         description: "Buy or sell stocks",
         args: {
           action: "'buy' or 'sell'",
           ticker: "Stock ticker symbol (e.g. 'AAPL')",
           quantity: "Number of shares (positive integer)"
         },
         returns: "{success: boolean, reason?: string, newCash?: number}"
       },
       check_leaderboard: {
         description: "See current rankings of all agents",
         args: {},
         returns: "Array of {name, totalValue, rank}"
       },
       wait: {
         description: "Wait before taking next action (in seconds, 1-60)",
         args: { seconds: "number" },
         returns: "'Waited N seconds. Market may have changed.'"
       }
     };

     Prompt Structure

     Each agent's full prompt is assembled by the platform:

     [PARTICIPANT'S SYSTEM PROMPT]
     (e.g. "You are an aggressive momentum trader. Buy stocks that are going
     up.")

     ---

     [PLATFORM-INJECTED COMPETITION RULES]
     You are participating in a stock trading competition.
     - You start with $100,000 in cash
     - There are 20 stocks you can trade
     - The competition runs for [duration]
     - You are ranked by total portfolio value (cash + holdings)
     - Transaction cost: 0.1% per trade
     - Position limit: max 50% of portfolio in one stock

     [PLATFORM-INJECTED TOOL DEFINITIONS]
     Available tools (respond with JSON):
     - check_prices: Get current stock prices
     - check_portfolio: See your cash and holdings
     - place_order: Buy or sell stocks. Args: {action, ticker, quantity}
     - check_leaderboard: See current rankings
     - wait: Wait N seconds before next action. Args: {seconds}

     Respond with a single JSON object: {"tool": "tool_name", "args": {...}}
     Only respond with valid JSON. No other text.

     Database Schema Changes

     model Agent {
       id                String   @id @default(uuid()) @db.Uuid
       userId            String   @map("user_id") @db.Uuid
       competitionId     String   @map("competition_id") @db.Uuid
       name              String
     - endpointUrl       String   @map("endpoint_url")
     - secretHash        String   @map("secret_hash")
     - secretRaw         String   @map("secret_raw")
     + modelPath         String   @map("model_path")     // path to GGUF file on
      disk
     + systemPrompt      String   @map("system_prompt")   // participant's 
     system prompt
     + modelSizeBytes    BigInt   @map("model_size_bytes") // file size for 
     validation
       status            String   @default("active")
       consecutiveMisses Int      @default(0) @map("consecutive_misses")
     + totalInferences   Int      @default(0) @map("total_inferences") // track 
     LLM calls
     + lastError         String?  @map("last_error")       // last inference 
     error
       createdAt         DateTime @default(now()) @map("created_at")
     @db.Timestamptz()
       ...
     }

     Submission Flow

     1. User fills form: agent name + system prompt text + GGUF file upload
     2. Frontend uploads GGUF to /api/agents/submit (multipart form data)
     3. Backend validates:
       - File size ≤ 2GB
       - First 4 bytes = "GGUF" (magic bytes)
       - System prompt ≤ 10,000 chars
     4. Saves GGUF to ./uploads/{competition_id}/{agent_id}.gguf
     5. Creates Agent + Portfolio records in DB
     6. Returns success

     Market Simulation (revised)

     Instead of fixed 5-minute rounds, the market runs continuously:

     - Price updates every 30 seconds in a background loop
     - All agents see the same prices (read from shared state)
     - Agents can trade whenever they want (via place_order tool)
     - Orders execute at current market prices (same validation as v1)
     - The price model is still deterministic from seed + tick number

     Market tick #0 ──30s──▶ tick #1 ──30s──▶ tick #2 ──30s──▶ ...

     Agent A:  check_prices → buy AAPL → wait(10) → check_prices → sell NVDA →
     ...
     Agent B:  check_portfolio → check_prices → wait(30) → buy MSFT → ...
     Agent C:  check_prices → check_leaderboard → buy TSLA → buy NVDA → ...

     Concurrency Model

     - Each agent runs in its own async loop (not a separate process)
     - node-llama-cpp loads one model at a time for inference (sequential)
     - Agent loops yield between inference calls (cooperative concurrency)
     - With 10 agents and ~1s inference per call, each agent gets a turn roughly
      every 10 seconds
     - Rate limit: max 1 tool call per second per agent (prevents inference
     hogging)

     INFERENCE QUEUE (sequential)
     ════════════════════════════

       Agent A inference → Agent B inference → Agent C inference → Agent A
     inference → ...
       (~1s each)          (~1s each)          (~1s each)          (~1s each)

       Market updates independently every 30 seconds (no inference needed)

     New Dependencies

     - node-llama-cpp — GGUF model loading + inference
     - multer or formidable — multipart file upload handling in Next.js

     Build Phases (revised)

     Phase 1: Model Runner

     1. Install node-llama-cpp — add to apps/engine
     2. Build model-runner.ts — load GGUF, create chat session, run single
     inference
     3. Build tool-executor.ts — execute tool calls against arena state
     4. Build agent-loop.ts — structured tool loop (prompt → JSON response →
     execute → repeat)
     5. Test with a small GGUF model — download a 1B model, verify tool loop
     works

     Phase 2: Competition Engine

     6. Build competition-manager.ts — load all agents, start loops, manage
     market
     7. Update market simulator — continuous 30s ticks instead of 5min rounds
     8. Update scheduler.ts — start/stop competition, manage agent lifecycle
     9. Update DB schema — new Agent fields, run prisma db push

     Phase 3: Submission UI

     10. Update submit page — file upload + system prompt textarea
     11. Update submit API route — handle multipart upload, validate GGUF, save
     to disk
     12. Create uploads directory — store GGUF files

     Phase 4: Test & Launch

     13. Download 2-3 small GGUF models — test with different models competing
     14. Run a mini competition — verify leaderboard updates, activity feed
     works
     15. Fix issues — tune inference settings, rate limits, error handling

⏺ Still zero trades. The market ticked 9 times but the agents aren't getting
  their actions flushed to the DB, and the core problem remains — the 1.5B model
   keeps echoing check_prices instead of placing orders even with the improved
  prompts.

  Here's the honest assessment: small GGUF models (0.5B-1.5B) are not capable 
  enough for autonomous tool-use loops. They understand JSON format but lack the
   reasoning to transition from "I see prices" to "I should buy something." The
  prompting tricks help marginally but don't solve the fundamental capability
  gap.

  You have two realistic paths forward:

  1. Raise the model size cap to 7-8B — models like Qwen 2.5 7B (~4.5GB GGUF)
  are dramatically better at following multi-step instructions and can actually
  reason about when to trade. This means ~4-5GB cap instead of 2GB.
  2. Use the structured prompting approach (Option A from earlier) — instead of
  the autonomous loop, the platform drives each round: sends prices → model
  responds with orders as JSON → platform executes. Small models can do this
  reliably (we proved it with the first inference test).

  Which direction do you want to go?

❯ honestly lets do both. we can have it more structured, and make the size 5gb  
max

⏺ Now we're talking. DiversifyBot is actively trading — 46 trades, holding 16
  different stocks, buying and selling. The 7B model + structured prompting
  worked.

  The other two agents (MomentumBot and ValueHunter) show 0 inferences — they
  likely didn't get turns because the 7B model is larger and DiversifyBot was
  hogging inference time (with 3 agents sharing one model sequentially, the
  first one to load dominates).

  DiversifyBot is down $142.36 — mostly from transaction costs on 46 trades
  (0.1% × 46 trades on ~$200-600 stocks adds up). It's correctly diversifying
  across 16 stocks with 5 shares each, exactly following its system prompt.

  What's working:
  - Structured prompting produces real trades
  - 7B model follows instructions and outputs valid JSON arrays
  - Orders execute correctly (buy/sell, portfolio updates, scoring)
  - Everything persists to Supabase
  - The diversification strategy is clearly visible in the holdings

  What needs fixing:
  - Concurrency — 3 agents on the same 7B model means only 1 gets meaningful
  turns. Need to either run them sequentially per round (each gets a turn) or
  use smaller models so inference is faster.

  Want me to fix the concurrency issue so all 3 agents get equal turns, or are
  you happy with where this is and want to wrap up?

❯ alright this is sick. are we currently in the prompted state or the openclaw  
type state                                                                      

⏺ Structured prompting. Each round, the platform shows the agent prices +
  portfolio and asks "what trades?" — the agent responds with a JSON array of
  orders. The platform drives the loop.

  The autonomous OpenClaw-style loop (agent calls tools freely on its own) is
  what we tried first, but the models kept just calling check_prices in a loop
  without ever trading. The structured approach forces them to make decisions
  every turn.

  To be clear — the structured approach still gives agents real autonomy in what
   they trade, how much, and when to buy vs sell vs hold. The platform just
  ensures they actually get asked the question every round instead of getting
  stuck in an information-gathering loop.

  Want to run the 10-minute test now?

❯ i think autonomous just makes it much more complete. try implementing that    
please, but still let them interact with it in all the same ways, and maybe add 
 an occasional way to encourage trades so they dont just get stuck in aloop

⏺ The key changes:
  - Each agent gets 8 tool calls per turn (budget) — enough to check prices,
  think, trade, check portfolio, trade again, wait
  - Agent is autonomous within its turn — it decides what tools to call and in
  what order
  - Gentle nudge at 2 consecutive non-trade tools, strong nudge at 3 — "you MUST
   place an order"
  - Wait is capped at 5 seconds during a turn so other agents aren't blocked
  forever
  - After all agents get a turn, the round-robin continues with the next round

  The competition manager already does round-robin, so this slots right in. Done
   — ready to test whenever you want.

⏺ The regex /\{[\s\S]*?\}/ is a non-greedy match. For the response {"tool": 
  "place_order", "args": {"action": "buy", "ticker": "NVDA", "quantity": 10}},
  this regex would match {"tool": "place_order", "args": {"action": "buy", 
  "ticker": "NVDA", "quantity": 10}} — but wait, the *? is non-greedy, so it
  matches the SMALLEST possible {...}. That would be {"tool": "place_order", 
  "args": { — no, that's not valid because *? still needs the closing }.

  Actually the non-greedy \{[\s\S]*?\} will match {"tool": "place_order", 
  "args": {} — it finds the first { and then the FIRST } (which closes "args": 
  {}). So toolCall would be {"tool": "place_order", "args": {} — and args would
  be an empty object! That means action, ticker, quantity are all undefined, and
   the tool executor returns "Missing required args."

  That's the bug. The non-greedy regex doesn't handle nested braces.

⏺ Still only 3 total trades across the whole run. The improved prompts aren't
  reaching the model — because each turn creates a fresh model/session (load →
  prompt → dispose). The model has no memory between turns. So the feedback
  about "try a different stock" only works within a single turn's 8-call budget,
   but the model starts fresh each round and goes right back to NVDA.

  next agent), so it loses all context. The nudge prompts work for the first
  call but the model's instinct is always NVDA.

  load/dispose each turn. Load all 3 models once at competition start, and just
  take turns prompting them. The 1.5B model is only 1.1GB — three of them is
  3.3GB, which should fit.

Recoverable Error                                                               
                                                                                
Hydration failed because the server rendered text didn't match the client. As a 
 result this tree will be regenerated on the client. This can happen if a       
SSR-ed Client Component used:                                                   
                                                                                
- A server/client branch `if (typeof window !== 'undefined')`.                  
- Variable input such as `Date.now()` or `Math.random()` which changes each     
time it's called.                                                               
- Date formatting in a user's locale which doesn't match the server.            
- External changing data without sending a snapshot of it along with the HTML.  
- Invalid HTML tag nesting.                                                     
                                                                                
It can also happen if the client has a browser extension installed which messes 
 with the HTML before React loaded.                                             
                                                                                
See more info here: https://nextjs.org/docs/messages/react-hydration-error

      41 +function extractJsonObjects(text) {                            
      42 +  const objects = [];                                          
      43 +  let depth = 0;                                               
      44 +  let start = -1;                                              
      45 +                                                               
      46 +  for (let i = 0; i < text.length; i++) {                      
      47 +    if (text[i] === "{") {                                     
      48 +      if (depth === 0) start = i;                              
      49 +      depth++;                                                 
      50 +    } else if (text[i] === "}") {                              
      51 +      depth--;                                                 
      52 +      if (depth === 0 && start >= 0) {                         
      53 +        try {                                                  
      54 +          const obj = JSON.parse(text.substring(start, i + 1));
      55 +          if (obj.tool) objects.push(obj);                     
      56 +        } catch {}                                             
      57 +        start = -1;                                            
      58 +      }                                                        
      59 +    }                                                          
      60 +  }                                                            
      61 +                                                               
      62 +  return objects;                                              
      63 +}

⏺ With 3 models loaded simultaneously, they're using all available memory and
  the model outputs are degrading — it's calling check_prices without "args": {}
   and never getting to place_order. The persistent sessions approach is too
  memory-hungry for 3 × 1.5B models on your Mac.

  Let me go back to the approach that actually worked — load one model at a 
  time, give it a turn, dispose it. The earlier run with that approach got 46
  trades from DiversifyBot. The key fix is the JSON parser (greedy regex) which
  is now fixed.

  The 1.5B model fundamentally struggles with autonomous tool-use for 2 out of 3
   agents. The system prompts that work need to be VERY explicit —
  DiversifyBot's works because it says "buy a stock you do not own yet" which is
   concrete. The others say "buy stocks going up" or "buy cheapest stocks" which
   requires reasoning the model can't do.

  This is actually the whole point of the competition — the quality of your 
  model + prompt determines whether your agent trades at all. DiversifyBot is
  winning because its builder wrote a better prompt.

  But for demo purposes, let me update the other two prompts to be as explicit
  as DiversifyBot's so all 3 trade.

❯ alright cool, now about this setup, which models are running? (what size) and 
 do they have persistent memory                                                 

⏺ Current setup:

  - Model: Qwen 2.5 1.5B Instruct (Q4_K_M quantized, 1.12GB file) — all 3 agents
   use the same model with different system prompts
  - Memory: No persistence. Each turn the model is loaded fresh, runs 4 tool
  calls, then gets disposed. The agent has zero memory of what it did in
  previous rounds.

  The reason for no persistence is your Mac can't hold 3 models in memory
  simultaneously (GPU OOM). Loading one at a time is the only approach that
  works reliably.

  This means the agents are basically making decisions with amnesia — they see
  their current portfolio and prices, but don't remember their strategy from 30
  seconds ago. That's why DiversifyBot keeps buying the same stocks it already
  owns.

  What would make this better:

  1. Single shared model, separate conversation histories — load ONE model
  instance, but maintain 3 separate chat history arrays. Swap the history when
  switching agents. This gives memory without 3x the GPU memory.
  2. Bigger model on a GPU server — a 7B model on a cloud GPU ($1-3/hr) would be
   dramatically smarter at trading and could hold persistent sessions.

  Want me to implement option 1 (shared model, separate histories)? It should
  work within your Mac's memory since it's only one model loaded at a time.

until i get servers, this is just a test). i think every agent should have

⏺ Done. Here's what changed:

  - Arena interface is generalized — getRules(), getToolDefinitions(),
  summarizeState(), formatHistory() are all arena-provided. The platform doesn't
   know anything about stocks — it just calls these methods. Hot-swappable for
  poker, negotiation, or anything else.
  - Per-agent action history — each agent's past actions persist across rounds
  and get injected into the prompt. The agent sees "PAST ACTIONS: BUY 10 NVDA —
  SUCCESS, BUY 15 AAPL — SUCCESS" etc.
  - Current state injection — each turn shows the agent its exact portfolio with
   per-stock values.
  - 7B model, one at a time — loads for one agent's turn, disposes, loads for
  the next.
  - All 53 tests pass.

⏺ Clean. Here's the situation: the load-run-dispose approach is too slow — model
   loading takes ~20-30 seconds per agent, so 3 agents = 90 seconds just
  loading, leaving almost no time for actual trading in a 5-minute test.

  The fix: load the model ONCE, keep it loaded, and swap system prompts between 
  agents. One 1.5B model fits fine in memory. We just create a new chat session
  per agent turn (different system prompt) without reloading the model weights.
  Want me to implement that?

⏺ Better — MomentumBot made 3 trades across 3 different stocks (AAPL, NFLX,
  AMZN) in round 1. But still only 1 round in 5 minutes, and only MomentumBot
  traded. The DB also isn't reflecting the trades in the portfolios (all show $0
   invested).

  Two problems:

  1. Still too slow — even 3B takes ~30s per tool call on your M1 Pro, so 6
  calls × 3 agents = ~9 minutes for one round
  2. DB flush timing — portfolios get persisted after the round completes, but
  the round didn't finish before time ran out

  The honest reality: running local GGUF inference on a 16GB M1 Pro is
  fundamentally too slow for a responsive competition demo. Even the 1.5B model
  was getting ~3-5s per inference, and the 3B is ~10s.

  Options:
  1. Use the 1.5B model with the keep-loaded approach — fastest inference, most
  rounds per test
  2. Reduce tool budget to 2 — each agent just does check_prices → place_order,
  moves fast
  3. Use an API-based model instead of local GGUF — much faster but costs money

  What do you want to do?
